通膨升溫壓抑美股表現，近期聚焦財報結果
2026 年 10 月 15 日
一、 市場動態
1. 科技股領跌，費半連 6 日收黑：美股主要指數 10 月 14 日全數下跌，道瓊工業指數下
跌 0.09%，收 51,621.48 點；S&P 500 指數下跌 1.11%，收 7,718.94 點，為 7 月 29
日以來最大單日跌幅；Nasdaq 綜合指數下跌 1.88%，收 26,692.87 點，為 7 月 23
日以來最大單日跌幅。費城半導體指數下跌 4.30%，收 11,684.38 點，連續第６個交
易日走低，為 2025 年 5 月以來最長連跌紀錄。

2. 類股非全面下挫，資金仍呈輪動現象：半導體及 AI 相關股票在 10/14 跌幅尤其明顯
，AMD 下跌 5.73%、Nvidia 下跌 3.16%、Broadcom 下跌 3.10%，AI 基礎建設相關
的 CoreWeave、Nebius、Oracle 及 Dell 跌幅更深，顯示市場對資本支出規模較大、
融資成本敏感度較高的 AI 相關企業風險偏好明顯下降。不過，S&P 500 的 11 大類
股中有 8 類下跌，資訊科技及通訊服務為主要拖累來源，但醫療保健、必需消費與能
源類股仍逆勢上漲。
3. 美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中急升後回落：美國 10 年期公債殖利率 10/14 由 5.2%左
右快速升至約 5.33%，逼近近期高點；然而，隨後市場消化 Fed 官員較審慎的政策訊
號，殖利率自高檔回落，終場約收於 5.19%，較前一交易日 5.21%小幅下降約 2 個基
點。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. 美 9 月通膨高於預期 ，Fed 再升息風險重新升高 ：美國 10/14 公布 9 月 CPI 月增 0.6%

、年增率也由 8 月的 3.4%上揚至 3.7%；核心 CPI 月增 0.3%、年增率由 8 月的 2.4%
升至 2.6%。其中汽油價格單月大漲 9.1%，貢獻整體 CPI 月增幅超過一半；住宅成本
亦月增 0.4%，顯示物價壓力仍具黏性。此數據公布後，市場重新提高 Fed 進一步升
息的預期 ，CME FedWatch 所反映的 10 月升息 1 碼機率一度由前一日約 22%提高至
37%。雖然截至 10/15 台北時間早上 7 點左右該機率回降至 29.3%，這也提醒投資人
Fed 的升息路徑仍存在不確定性。
2. 高利率同時壓縮科技股估值與提高 AI 融資成本 ：科技及半導體公司未來獲利成長占評
價的比重較高，因此對折現率變化相對敏感。當長天期美債殖利率維持 5%以上，市
場願意支付的本益比自然受到限制，即使企業獲利仍維持成長，股價也可能因評價倍
數下降而出現修正。另一方面，本輪 AI 投資需要大量資料中心、伺服器、電力與網路
基礎設施，高利率亦直接增加企業融資成本，反映信用市場對高度依賴外部資金的 AI

基礎建設公司要求更高風險溢酬。

策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
3. AI 投資焦點由「支出規模」轉向「投資報酬率」:近期市場對 AI 的疑慮逐漸由「需求
是否成長」 ，轉向「龐大的資本支出能否轉化為足夠營收、獲利與自由現金流」 。
Broadcom 執行長陳福陽近期亦指出 ，AI 未來發展的重要限制可能逐漸由晶片或記憶
體供應轉向資本本身。我們認為，這並不代表AI 資本支出週期即將結束，而是市場已
由第一階段的 「資本支出愈大、成長想像愈高」 ，逐步進入更重視資本效率與投資報酬
率的第二階段。未來財務結構、現金流與AI 商業化能力，將成為不同企業評價分化的
重要因素。
4. 中國記憶體競爭疑慮加重半導體短線壓力：中國長鑫存儲（CXMT）10/10 傳出記憶

體技術取得進展 ， 引發市場對未來DRAM 供給增加及價格競爭的疑慮 ， 記憶體相關類
股率先承壓，並進一步影響整體半導體市場情緒。惟 DRAM 與 GPU、客製化 ASIC、
先進晶圓製程及先進封裝的產業競爭與需求結構並不相同。我們認為，儘管記憶體競
爭升溫值得關注，但目前尚不足以延伸為整體 AI 半導體需求同步轉弱。
5. 高檔籌碼加速獲利了結：AI 與半導體仍是 2026 年美股表現最強勢的投資主題之一，
部分科技股今年累計漲幅已相當可觀。當通膨、殖利率及AI 投資報酬疑慮同時升高，
先前漲幅較大、投資人部位較集中的族群自然成為優先調節對象。因此，本波費半快
速修正除了總經與基本面疑慮外，亦包含籌碼降溫及風險部位收縮因素，使短期價格
波動幅度可能高於企業基本面實際變化。
三、市場看法
1. 類股輪動明顯，尚非全面看空美股：科技與 AI 相關股票 10 月 14 日明顯承壓，但醫
療保健、必需消費與能源類股仍逆勢上漲，道瓊工業指數僅下跌 0.09%。此外，S&P
500 成分股下跌家數與上漲家數之比約為 1.1：1；相較於指數下跌 1.11%，市場廣度

並未同步大幅惡化。這反映資金主要由高評價且前期漲幅較大的科技與AI 族群，轉向
現金流較穩定的防禦型產業。現階段市場特徵較接近類股輪動與風格切換，而非無差
別的全面風險撤離，亦尚未看到美股企業獲利或經濟基本面全面反轉的明確訊號。
2. 美股科技股財報前風險降載，Q3 仍存在重新定價空間：近期科技股修正，費城半導
體指數連續６個交易日下跌，也可能部分反映第三季財報季正式展開前，投資人在股
價先前漲幅較大、評價偏高及利率環境不確定下，提前降低風險曝險。真正決定後續
市場方向的關鍵 ， 仍在於實際企業獲利能否支持目前的高成長預期 。根據 Bloomberg
季度獲利成長追蹤資料，今年 Q1 與 Q2 財報季期間的 EPS 成長率經驗顯示，隨實際
財報陸續公布，企業獲利成長曾明顯優於財報季開始前的市場預期。儘管這並不保證
Q3 將複製前兩季的上修幅度，但目前市場因財報不確定性所反映的風險折價仍存在
被實際財報重新驗證的可能。若大型科技及半導體公司後續營收、獲利與AI 需求指引
實際公布結果優於預期，近期風險溢價可望部分收斂；反之，若獲利成長無法跟上目

前評價，市場整理時間則可能進一步延長。

不另行通知。
3. 費半修正幅度大，但 AI 需求尚未出現全面反轉：ASML 在 10/14 所公布的最新第三
季財報反而顯示 AI 相關設備需求仍相當強勁，該公司表示 2027 與 2028 年規劃產能
可能仍無法完全滿足客戶需求，部分客戶亦已表達對 2029 及 2030 年設備需求的興
趣。儘管費城半導體指數近期市場情緒轉弱，但由 ASML 的財報表現來看，目前仍未
看到大型 AI 客戶全面取消晶片訂單、主要雲端服務商同步削減 AI 資本支出，或先進
製程需求快速降溫等結構性訊號。
4. 台積電營收顯示 AI 需求暢旺 ， 留意10/15 法說會內容：台積電 10/8 公布的第三季營

收達新台幣 1.49 兆元、年增約 50%，創歷史新高，並高於市場原先預估的 1.46 兆元
；9 月單月營收年增 54.6%。強勁的營收表現仍反映 AI 與先進製程需求具有支撐。目
前市場因此呈現「企業營收仍快速成長，但股票評價下降」的狀況。高殖利率環境下
，EPS 上升與本益比下降可以同時發生，後續股價方向將取決於企業獲利上修幅度是
否足以抵銷評價收縮。該公司將於 10/15 下午舉行第三季法人說明會。市場重點將集
中於第四季展望、AI 與 HPC 需求、先進製程、先進封裝及未來資本支出規劃。公司
對 AI 需求與產能投資的最新判斷，將成為判斷近期費半修正主要屬於評價調整，或產
業基本面是否開始出現變化的重要依據。
5. AI 與半導體進入基本面分化階段：我們認為，在高利率環境下，高度仰賴外部融資、
自由現金流尚未成熟的 AI 基礎建設業者 ， 受到的資金成本壓力明顯高於具穩定現金流
的大型科技與雲端服務商。同樣地，記憶體、GPU、ASIC、先進製程及先進封裝各有
不同供需結構。我們預期下一階段 AI 行情將更加重視訂單能見度、產品競爭力、毛利

率、自由現金流及 AI 營收實際兌現，產業內部強弱分化程度可能進一步提高。
6. Fed 路徑仍存不確定性，惟企業獲利可望提供股市支撐：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使市場重
新評估 Fed 後續升息時點與幅度，美國利率政策仍存在不確定性，長天期美債殖利率
也可能維持高檔。不過，現階段企業獲利成長與AI 相關需求仍具韌性，若第三季財報

不另行通知。
延續前兩季優於預期的趨勢，企業 EPS 若能上修有望部分抵銷高利率對本益比的壓縮
效果。換言之，後續市場表現未必只由 Fed 政策主導，企業實際獲利能否持續兌現，
將是支撐科技與半導體股中期走勢的重要力量。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦美股財報與 Fed 決策，科技股中期趨勢料不致有變：近期市場同時面臨財報
前的觀望態勢、美債殖利率偏高及 AI 資本支出疑慮等不確定因素，短線風險偏好可能
受到壓抑，後續宜關注 10 月美股財報對企業獲利與資本支出展望，以及 FOMC 會議
所釋出的政策訊號。中期而言，目前AI 相關企業營運與資本支出尚未出現全面轉弱跡

象，科技產業基本面仍具一定支撐，但在利率維持高檔及市場評價偏高的環境下，後
續宜更加重視企業獲利、現金流及資本支出的實際變化。
2. 中天期債券風險報酬仍較均衡 ：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使短端殖利率持續受到 Fed 政策路
徑影響，而 20～30 年超長端仍面臨財政赤字、國債供給與期限溢價偏高等結構性壓
力。相較之下，目前殖利率已位於歷史相對高檔，若後續核心通膨或能源價格改善，
中段殖利率具有回落條件，同時其存續期間風險又低於超長端，因此現階段以中天期
美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對較佳。

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 美國 9 月 ISM 非製造業 PMI 從 8 月的 55.4 降至 54.9，顯示服務業活動增速放
緩，然而國內需求強勁使供應鏈承壓，並推動投入價格指數由 72.6 升至 74.0 為
四年多來新高；另一方面，美國 10 月密西根大學消費者信心指數由 9 月的 48.1
降至 46.3，為連續第三個月下降且創 5 月以來最低 ， 惟未來一年通膨預期由 4.6%
升至 4.7%，顯示通膨可能持續維持高位至 2027 年。
⚫ 聯準會 9 月 FOMC 會議紀要顯示與會官員均支持升息，並認為年底前尚需升息
一次 ，但對於後續升息時點及升息理由上存在分歧； 其中部分官員認為升息是防
止能源及其他價格衝擊造成更廣泛影響 的預防性措施，但更為鷹派官員則認為
升息在於抑制投資和支出，以防範正在顯現的需求驅動型通膨。
⚫ 歐洲央行 9 月貨幣政策會議紀要顯示全體決策委員均同意升息，並認為當前經
濟成長風險偏向下行 ， 通膨風險則偏向上行 ，因此將保留政策彈性， 根據經濟數

據做為決策，不提供未來利率路徑指引。
⚫ 台灣 9 月 CPI 年增率自 8 月 2.05%上升至 2.73%，已連續 5 個月超過 2%通膨警
戒線 ， 且創兩年半來最大漲幅 ，除了豪雨導致蔬果價格上漲及中秋假期的短期因
素外，油價居高不下亦使通膨壓力增加。
⚫ 中國央行發布匯率政策立場聲明， 強調中國貿易發展根源於產業國際競爭力的
提升，無必要通過匯率貶值獲取貿易競爭優勢 ，並指出中國長期實施以市場供需
為基礎、參考一籃子貨幣進行調節管理浮動匯率制度。

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.699，低點在 31.940，以 31.875 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 4.5 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶，週初，受到前週公布的美國非農就業數據低於預期，降低
市場對聯準會 10 月升息的預期， 台股創高吸引外資匯入 ， 推升台幣最高來到31.699；
不過隨著美伊緊張情勢升溫且台灣連假將至 ， 台股收低使外資轉為大幅賣超並匯出 ，
推低台幣回到 9 月中以來的震盪區間下緣，最低觸及 31.940，最終在央行調節之下
收在 31.875。 
＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國 10 月密西根大學消費者信心
指數持續下滑，由 9 月的 48.1 降至 46.3，創 5 月以來最低，惟未來一年通膨預期
升至 4.7%，加上中東局勢反覆，油價高位震盪，仍支撐市場對於聯準會年內升息
的預期，預計美元指數維持在 102 附近震盪，對台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，
台灣 9 月出口創歷史新高，年增 60.9%，反映 AI 及科技需求持續強勁，出口商後
續拋匯需求可望提供台幣支撐；然而，台股逼近五萬點關卡之際，外資未能延續買
超走勢，且連假前已大幅賣超台股，若後續外資持續匯出，將限制台幣升值空間。
綜合以上，預估本週台幣區間震盪。

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY 震盪走升。週初，因美元指數在102 附近盤整，帶領USD/JPY 延
續前一週走勢震盪走升到 158 上方；週間，因缺乏重要經濟數據，市場偏向觀望，
USD/JPY 維持在 158 附近震盪；臨近週末，USD/JPY 曾一度回測 157.50 附近，不

漲 84 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、路透短觀調查顯示，受半導
體強勁訂單推動，日本 10 月大型製造業信心升至正 22，創近五年新高；但原料成
本高漲重擊非製造業，信心指數降至正 23。與此同時，此前投下升息反對票的日銀
鴿派委員佐藤綾野立場軟化，表態支持分階段升息，市場對後續正常化預期升溫，
預計將限縮 USD/JPY 的上行空間 。二 、 日本首相高市早苗表示密切關注匯率與物價
走勢 ， 強調日本已脫離通縮 ， 將完全尊重央行政策獨立性 ， 暗示不反對進一步升息 。
面對通膨加劇與日圓走貶壓力，其過往堅持的貨幣寬鬆立場已出現轉向。然而目前
美日實質利差仍大，持續為 USD/JPY 提供支撐。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，
USD/JPY 持續沿著上彎的月線上攻，然匯價持續承壓於季線下方，在有效站上季線
及突破前高壓力前，仍以區間震盪看待。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震
盪的可能性較高。

險需求 ， 加上美債殖利率維持高檔 ， 美元利差優勢延續，歐元維持低檔； 臨近週末 ，

＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間偏空震盪，主要原因如下：一、歐洲央行首席經濟學
家連恩表示，夏末能源成本飆升為經濟增長帶來下行風險，並可能限制央行收緊貨
幣政策的力道，與此同時，歐元區 10 月 Sentix 投資者信心指數由 5.1 降至 2.7，德
國預期指數同步滑落，反映市場對經濟復甦的信心明顯降溫，預計將令歐元持續承
壓。二、法國社會動盪與支出訴求阻礙財政減赤進程，加劇債務可持續性疑慮，並
推升法德公債利差與信用風險，令歐元承壓走弱。相較美國經濟展現韌性且聯準會
維持偏鷹立場，歐洲深陷景氣放緩與財政掣肘，歐洲央行緊縮空間受限。在歐美基
本面與政策前景持續分歧下 ，EUR/USD 缺乏實質支撐 ， 後市將維持偏弱整理並下探
關鍵支撐。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價雖多次向下測試 1.1165 關
鍵價位均獲得支撐，惟每次反彈均無法站上五日線，且各天期均線下彎呈現空頭排
列。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間偏空震盪的可能性較高。

### Page 1

Summary: After market hours on 5 October 2026, EMC, TUC, and ITEQ released their 
monthly sales results. Our read-through is positive as the three key CCL vendors beat 
sales expectations by 5-15% on better pricing and on-going spec upgrades. Our thesis is 
that the strong AI demand is causing a spill-over impact on the whole CCL industry with 
low-end CCL having c.100% price hike in 2026E and high-end CCL seeing a 40-50% price 
hike due to cost inflation in 2026E. Recent data points showed this thesis remains valid. 
Looking ahead, we expect high-end/low-end CCL to see additional c.10%/20% ASP 
increases in 4Q26E, which could be the key share price catalyst. We believe that recent 
negatives news (including the impact of PTFE and CPO/NPO on the industry) are 
overdone. The impact on high-end CCL from these new technologies is likely to be limited 
from the point of view of Taiwan CCL companies. As such, we remain positive that the 
three names in our CCL coverage will ride on the decent price hike trend given favourable 
supply-demand dynamics.

Copper clad laminate (CCL) sector: Sep-26 monthly sales (TWD bn)

• EMC (2383 TT, TWD5,700, Buy [1]) 
EMC’s September 2026 revenue was TWD21.5bn (+7% MoM, +169% YoY), which led to 
its 3Q26 sales (TWD60.9bn; +29% QoQ, +142% YoY) reaching 112% of our 3Q26 forecast 
(TWD54.4bn) and 110% of the Bloomberg consensus forecast (TWD55.5bn). We attribute 
the revenue strength to the higher-than-expected price hike and on-going spec upgrades 
(leading to higher ASPs). Management attributes c.50% of the MoM increase in revenue in 
September to the price hike and the other 50% came to product mix optimisation. Looking 
ahead, the company doesn’t rule out the potential to increase its ASP again in 4Q26 (after 
the first time in 2Q26) in order to reflect rising raw material prices since 2Q26. As such, we 
expect revenue to grow at least high teens again on the potential price head as well as 
ongoing spec upgrades.

EMC sees limited impact from PTFE and HC glass-fibre-free CCL. Based on our 
understanding, PTFE is still under certification accounting for only limited layers inside 
specified high-end PCB as of now. As such, its impact on the high-end CCL TAM should be 
limited. As for HC glass-fibre-free CCL, the rigidity of PCB using this type of CCL could be 
a key concern to us.

Lastly, regarding the recent Co-Packaged Optics/Near-Packaged Optics (CPO/NPO) 
news’s negative impact on the CCL TAM, we also believe it to be limited given the likely 
adoption date for CPO of 2030, based on EMC’s forecast. Besides, we believe the PCB 
board inside CPO scale-up switches at that time would still need to support an 800G/1.6T 
transmission speed (or even lower) which would still require high-end PCB transmission 
although the highest transmission bandwidth can increase to 3.2T via optics. Lastly, EMC 
believes the spec of CCL won’t downgrade to M4 in the worst-case scenario but would be 
at least M6. Last but not least, we believe this kind of CPO-connected AI server might only

Given its leading position in the CCL industry and the likely imbalance between supply and 
demand in 2027, we remain positive view on EMC. We have a Buy (1) rating on EMC, with

• TUC (6274 TT, TWD1,780, Buy [1]) 
TUC’s September 2026 revenue was TWD6.1bn (+4% MoM, +115% YoY), which led to its 
3Q26 sales (TWD17.9bn; +26% QoQ, +123% YoY) reaching 103% of our 3Q26 forecast 
(TWD17.4bn) and 105% of the Bloomberg consensus forecast (TWD17.1bn). Looking 
ahead, we expect TUC to deliver at least QoQ growth in 4Q26 given 1) a price hike along 
with its peers for high-end CCL (eg. M8) and 2) some shipment delay from 3Q26 given 
flooding in Southeast Asia. For the longer term, the company is promising a continual spec 
upgrade trend in 2027 with new projects using M9-grade CCL (including low dk1, low dk2, 
and Q-glass version) in 2027 for high-end switches, AI servers, and optical transceivers. 
Considering the insufficient capacity in the CCL industry, we expect TUC to further optimise 
its product mix after acquiring more high-end CCL orders in future quarters (vs. a single 
major AI server client in 2026). We have a Buy (1) rating on TUC, with a 12-month TP of 
TWD2,320, based on a target PER of 35x applied to our 1-year forward EPS forecast. Key 
downside risks: 1) weaker-than-expected demand for switches/AI servers, and 2) market 
share loss to CCL peers.

• ITEQ (6213 TT, TWD751, Buy [1]) 
ITEQ’s September 2026 revenue was TWD5.8bn (+15% MoM, +111% YoY), which led to 
its 3Q26 sales (TWD15.6bn; +35% QoQ, +102% YoY) reaching 114% of our 3Q26 forecast 
(TWD13.7bn) and 108% of the Bloomberg consensus forecast (TWD14.5bn). The number 
for 3Q26 was higher than our estimate due likely to a stronger-than-expected price hike 
(c.30% in 3Q26 vs. expectation of 20%). Looking ahead to 4Q26, we expect the company 
to further raise its pricing by 20-30% to factor in cost inflation in raw materials (E-glass) 
and the supply-demand gap. The company also expects the rising supply of e-glass (which 
could lift its utilisation rate from the current level of 75%) to lift its gross margin more 
significantly. For 2027, the company is excited about the new CPU adoption driving rising 
M7 CCL usage and is working on M9 certification with new end customers, which could 
become a key growth driver in 2028. We have a Buy (1) rating on ITEQ. Key downside 
risk: weaker-than-expected regular server demand.

Document 082: 261006_daiwa_winway

### Page 1

Summary: Winway reported September revenue of TWD1.25bn (-26.7% MoM; +87.7% 
YoY), brining 3Q26 revenue to a record high of TWD4.56bn (+29.3% QoQ; +152.6% YoY). 
The 3Q26 top-line result beat market consensus by 6.7% but missed Daiwa’s estimate by 
9.1%. Following August’s exceptionally strong top line (TWD1.71bn), we had factored in a 
higher shipment run-rate for September. However, the miss stems primarily from the 
lumpiness of probe-card deliveries (high concentration to a single customer) and customer 
qualification timelines, rather than order cancellations or share loss.

We forecast 4Q26 revenue to track flattish to slightly down QoQ, reflecting lower probe-
card shipment volumes compared to 3Q26, alongside typical year-end pull-in deceleration 
from customers. On profitability, we maintain our view that 2Q26 marked the trough for 
gross margin this year. As the revenue contribution from lower-margin MEMS probe cards 
declines, a more favourable product mix should drive a steady QoQ gross margin recovery, 
which may drive gross margin to reach 40% earlier than market expectations.

Although we remain positive on Winway’s structural growth profile, near-term catalysts 
appear muted. This reflects the revenue lumpiness in probe cards, while additional socket 
capacity will not come online until March 2027—pushing the corresponding revenue 
recognition into 2Q27. We will revisit our financial model upon the company’s 3Q26 results 
release. We currently have a Buy (1) rating on Winway with a 12-month TP of TWD10,000.

Source PDF: all report pdf/[329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

00

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 
기다렸던 4 행정 중속 엔진 캐파 증설 단행 
HD현대중공업의 4행정 엔진 캐파는 기존 3.0GW에서 증설이 완료되는 '28년부터 
램프업을 거쳐 '30년 7.2GW까지 확대될 계획이다. ①[육상 발전용]은 신공장(울산 
온산) 3GW 와 HD 현대엔진(전남 영암) 1GW 를 합쳐 4.0GW, ②[선박용]은 
2.3GW에서 3.2GW로 확대된다. HD현대엔진 캐파는 기존 0.7GW에서 1GW까
지 확대되며, 해당 라인은 신공장이 가동되기 전 ‘27~’28 년 구간에서의 선박&육상 엔
진 모두 대응 가능하다. 신공장 캐파는 총 3.0GW로, ‘28년 약 30%의 가동률로 시
작해 램프업을 거쳐 ‘30 년부터 풀캐파로 20MW급 모델을 생산할 예정이다. 4 행정 
중속엔진에 대한 수요 가시성은 높다. 동사가 공유했던 육상 발전 엔진 인콰이어리 규
모는 기존 약 3~9GW였으나, 현재는 이를 크게 상회하는 수준으로 소통하고 있다.

최근 부유식 데이터센터(FDC)에 대한 하이퍼스케일러들의 인콰이어리 또한 확대되고 
있다. HD 한국조선해양은 최근 미국 선급(ABS)로부터 100MW급 규모의 부유식 발
전 설비(BMPP)에 대한 기본 인증(AiP)를 획득했으며, 동사는 FDC 신조 사업과 더불
어 발전원 4 행정 엔진을 파워십 및 부유식 발전설비(BMPP)에 납품하며 그룹사들과 
패키지 사업으로 진행할 예정이다. 가시화된 수요를 기반으로 엔진기계사업부의 구조적 
성장 보여줄 것으로 전망하며, 이에 따른 리레이팅을 예상한다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원 유지 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원을 유지한다. 동사는 
올해 상선수주 목표 이미 초과달성하며 안정적인 펀더멘털 유지 중이다. 하반기 남은 
기간은 LNGC, VLGC/VLAC 중심의 선별 수주 전략을 통해 ‘28 년 이후의 상선 부
문 수익성 유지 충분히 가능할 것으로 예상된다. 글로벌 지정학적 리스크 확대에 따른 
해군 현대화 사이클 기조에 맞추어 미 해군은 동맹국의 호위함 구매를 고려하고 있으
며, MASGA를 기반으로 중장기적으로 미국 전투함을 건조하기 위해 HD현대의 미국 
진출 가능성 높다는 판단이다. 또한 미국을 제외한 글로벌 함정(페루, 필리핀, 말레이시
아, 칠레, 사우디, 이집트 등) 수주 파이프라인도 다수 보유하고 있어 함정 모멘텀에 따
른 리레이팅 충분히 가능할 전망이다. 특수선+4 행정 엔진+FDC+SMR 등 조선소에 
붙일 수 있는 모든 추가 성장동력을 보유한 유일한 종목이기에 조선업 최선호주(Top-
Pick)로 제시한다. 최근 불거지고 있는 노조 파업 문제 해소될 시 저점 매수 기회라는 
판단이다.

2026-09-17

조선/방산. 한승한, R.A 고서영 / shane.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-9992

Document 160: AI融资隐忧重压芯片股，全球科技板块本周已蒸发逾万亿美元

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI融资隐忧重压芯片股，全球科技板块本周已蒸发逾万亿美元.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

美联储加息预期升温、 AI 融资成本攀升等多重压⼒叠加，本周全球科技股遭遇⼤规模抛售
—— 全球前 20 ⼤ AI 及半导体股市值已经合计蒸发逾 1 万亿美元，半导体 ETF （ SOXX ）创 11
年最⻓连跌纪录。周四， SK 海⼒⼠单⽇继续暴跌逾 10% ，英伟达、三星、软银⽆⼀幸免。
摘要：
加息预期、 AI 融资成本攀升与竞争格局恶化，多重压⼒在本周同步发酵，将全球科技股推⼊⼀场
⼤规模抛售。
周四全球科技股的抛售仍在继续，美股⽅⾯，英伟达当⽇收跌 1.84% ， AMD 跌 1.5% ，英特尔跌
约 2.8% ，戴尔科技重挫 6.8% 。亚洲市场跌幅更为惨烈， SK 海⼒⼠单⽇暴跌逾 10% ，三星电⼦下
跌 8.73% ，⽇本软银集团⼤跌 7.9% 。
据 CNBC 统计，截⾄本周四，全球市值最⾼的 20 只 AI 及半导体股票合计蒸发逾 1 万亿美元。追踪
芯⽚板块的 iShares 半导体 ETF （ SOXX ）周四录得连续第⼋个交易⽇下跌，区间累计跌幅约
12.7% ，据道琼斯市场数据，这是该 ETF 11 年来最⻓的连跌纪录。

英伟达 CEO ⻩仁勋在斯德哥尔摩出席活动时向彭博表⽰，他认为英伟达⽆需为 CoreWeave 提供
背书，并称 " 他们的资本成本实际上正在下降 " 。这番表态带动新云计算股（ neocloud ）盘中反
弹， CoreWeave 收涨 5.94% ， Nebius 上涨 1.29% 。
多重利空叠加，抛售逻辑层层递进
本轮抛售并⾮单⼀事件触发，⽽是多条负⾯线索在同⼀时间窗⼝内相互强化。
通胀数据是导⽕索。 周三公布的美国 9 ⽉消费者价格指数（ CPI ）⾼于预期，市场随即加⼤对美
联储进⼀步加息的押注。纳斯达克综合指数周三收跌 1.88% ⾄ 26692.87 点，标普 500 指数跌
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

1.11% ⾄ 7718.94 点。
Anthropic 数据令市场情绪雪上加霜。 据英国《⾦融时报》报道， AI 初创公司 Anthropic 在周三
⼀场私下投资者会议上披露，第三季度营收达 168 亿美元，环⽐增⻓约 50%—— 但这⼀增速远低
于第⼆季度约 145% 的环⽐增幅。 D.A. Davidson 分析师 Gil Luria 在客⼾报告中写道： " 市场似乎
将 Anthropic 的更新解读为负⾯信号，考虑到市场本就对 AI 交易⾼度敏感，这种反应可以理解。 "
Luria 本⼈对此持更为中性的看法。他指出， Anthropic 第三季度实现正向现⾦流，这表明该公司
和 OpenAI" 并⾮天⽣⽆法盈利的商业模式 " 。他还写道，从更宏观的 AI ⾏业来看， " 整体 AI 使⽤量
（以及 AI ⽀出）未必出现减速 " 。
中国竞争同步发酵。  ⻓鑫存储上周末宣布，在新架构上取得突破，可在更⼩空间内存储更多数
据。埃隆 · ⻢斯克随即在 X 平台发⽂称，中国将在 2028 年⽣产 " ⼤量内存芯⽚ " 。

与此同时，博通和英特尔的⾸席执⾏官本周⼀均警告称，融资问题可能成为 AI 扩张的重⼤阻
⼒，博通 CEO Hock Tan 表⽰，这⼀挑战甚⾄可能超过芯⽚或电⼒短缺。
利率上⾏，直接冲击 AI 基础设施投资回报
⾼利率环境对 AI 基础设施建设的冲击，是本轮抛售的核⼼逻辑之⼀。
投⾏ Post Oak Group 执⾏董事 James Vrachas 向 MarketWatch 表⽰，芯⽚股已经 " 消化了⼤量未
来增⻓预期 " ，股价本⾝已反映这些预期。在此背景下， " 情绪的轻微转变或对⽀出的质疑，都会
对股价产⽣显著冲击 " 。他说： " 相信 AI 的⻓期潜⼒，和相信每⼀家受益于这⼀增⻓的公司都被合
理定价，是两回事。 "
Baird 投资策略师 Ross Mayfield 向 MarketWatch 指出，国债收益率上⾏对芯⽚板块构成压⼒，尤
其是⾼成⻓股以及 "AI ⻛险谱系中更偏激进⼀端 " 、更依赖外部融资的公司。他表⽰， CPI 数据可
能加⼤美联储加息的紧迫性， " 这对短端利率造成巨⼤压⼒，对那些财务基础较弱、或持有与短

期利率挂钩的浮动利率债务的借款⼈打击尤为直接 " 。
Mayfield 还指出，即便是超⼤规模云服务商（ hyperscaler ）也并⾮对利率完全免疫。 " 即使是最
⼤、现⾦最充裕的公司，也必须重新评估这些项⽬的投资回报率， " 他说， " ⽽市场的⼤部分动能
都建⽴在超⼤规模云服务商对硬件和数据中⼼基础设施的⽀出之上，这就给整条供应链蒙上了
⼀层阴影。 "
亚欧市场跌幅更深，韩⽇股市⾸当其冲
美股的抛售情绪迅速蔓延⾄亚洲和欧洲市场，且跌幅普遍超过美股。
周四，韩国 SK 海⼒⼠单⽇暴跌逾 10% ，三星电⼦跌 8.73% ， LG Innotek 跌 8.95% ，⾸尔半导体
跌 9.28% ，三星 SDI 跌 6.26% 。韩国综合股价指数（ KOSPI ）逼近 4 ⽉以来最低收盘⽔平。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
⽇本市场同样重挫。软银集团跌 7.9% ，半导体测试设备商爱德万测试（ Advantest ）暴跌近
14% ，芯⽚制造设备供应商东京电⼦跌 6.45% ，瑞萨电⼦跌 8% 。台积电跌 1.81% 。
欧洲⽅⾯，荷兰半导体设备巨头 ASML ⼩幅下跌，英⻜凌跌 5% ， ASM International 跌 7% 。
分析师：需求信号才是真正的警报线
尽管市场情绪低迷，部分分析师认为本轮抛售尚未触及 AI 需求的实质性恶化。
Yorkville Ives 科技分析师 Dan Ives 在报告中写道，本轮抛售反映的是市场对融资成本的担忧，⽽
⾮对 AI 算⼒需求的质疑。 " 美国国债收益率升破 5% ，确实推⾼了这些基础设施项⽬的融资成本，
投资者关注资产负债表和合同结构是合理的，但利率是融资问题，不是需求问题。 "

Ives 认为当前是买⼊机会，并表⽰ AI 基础设施热潮仍处于早期阶段。他明确列出了真正值得警惕
的信号： " 真正会改变我们判断的，是需求信号，⽽不是利率信号 "—— 包括超⼤规模云服务商削
减资本⽀出计划、合同取消或 GPU 租赁价格下跌。
Gabelli Funds 投资组合经理 Hendi Susanto 则向 MarketWatch 表⽰，许多芯⽚及半导体设备公
司 " 资产负债表依然稳健，⾃由现⾦流充裕 " ，他预计其中⼤多数公司 " 将以强劲态势收官今年 " ，
尤其是那些需求仍超过供给的公司。他还指出，即将到来的科技公司财报季将揭⽰ " 投资者在多
⼤程度上将通胀担忧纳⼊定价 " 。

Document 161: AI资本开支暗藏“租赁账单”！摩根士丹利将2027年投资额上调至1.5万亿美元

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI资本开支暗藏“租赁账单”！摩根士丹利将2027年投资额上调至1.5万亿美元.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

⼤摩表⽰，若将未体现在现⾦流中的租赁资本开⽀纳⼊统计， 2027 年全球 AI 计算投资总规
模将升⾄ 1.58 万亿美元，较现⾦⼝径扩⼤ 14% 。⾼额投资将⼤幅驱动标普 500 营收与盈
利，其中英伟达独占约三分之⼆流⼊份额。不过，融资压⼒正加剧⾼低评级主体分化，低
评级云服务商⾯临更⼤的融资约束与成本敏感性。
摘要：
当传统资本开⽀⼝径不再完整，租赁融资正悄然重塑 AI 基础设施投资的全貌。
据追⻛交易台消息，据摩根⼠丹利 Nicholas Lentini 团队的最新研究报告，将租赁资本开⽀纳⼊
统计后，超⼤规模云服务商（ hyperscaler ）及新兴云服务商（ neocloud ） 2027 年 AI 计算投资
总规模升⾄约 1.58 万亿美元，较现⾦资本开⽀⼝径扩⼤约 14% 。这⼀数字意味着 2026 年⾄ 2027
年间， AI 投资增量⾼达约 7000 亿美元，增速约 60% 。
摩根⼠丹利指出，这 1.58 万亿美元的资本开⽀将直接带动标普 500 指数约 5% 的 2027 年营收，并

贡献约 25% 的营收增速。更⼴泛看，被摩根⼠丹利归类为 AI 核⼼受益者的 " 使能者 " （ Enablers ）
与 " 采⽤者 " （ Adopters ）合计将驱动标普 500 指数 78% 的 2027 年盈利增⻓。值得注意的是，英伟
达⼀家公司预计将独占标普 500 指数 AI 资本开⽀流⼊规模的约三分之⼆。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

研究显⽰，超⼤规模云服务商已披露逾 1.1 万亿美元尚未启动的租赁承诺。由于数据中⼼租赁属
于债务融资性质的资产购置，在现⾦流量表中不体现为资本开⽀，亦不进⼊管理层指引，导致
市场⻓期低估 AI 实际投资规模。将租赁项⽬纳⼊后，摩根⼠丹利估计 2026 年、 2027 年、 2028 年
AI 总资本开⽀分别约为 9800 亿美元、 1.58 万亿美元及 1.81 万亿美元，同⽐增速分别约为 100% 、
60% 及 14% 。
租赁资本开⽀： " 影⼦账单 " 有多⼤
摩根⼠丹利在本次报告中⾸次系统引⼊超⼤规模云服务商的租赁资本开⽀预测。根据其估算，
2027 年租赁资本开⽀达 1950 亿美元， 2028 年进⼀步扩⼤⾄ 2460 亿美元。从单家公司看， 2027
年甲⻣⽂贡献最⼤，达 572 亿美元，其次依次为微软（ 453 亿美元）、 META （ 372 亿美元）、亚⻢
逊（ 284 亿美元）及⾕歌（ 266 亿美元）。
这⼀问题的核⼼在于会计处理⽅式。超⼤规模云服务商通过租赁⽅式获取数据中⼼的⾏为，在

经济实质上等同于债务融资购置资产，但并不体现在现⾦流量表的资本开⽀科⽬下，也不纳⼊
管理层的资本开⽀指引。摩根⼠丹利认为，这导致传统资本开⽀预测 " 越来越难以还原资本投⼊
的全貌 " 。
从投资回报⻆度看，摩根⼠丹利对这批投资持乐观态度。报告估计，新增计算容量的投资回报
率（ ROIC ）在 25% ⾄ 50% 之间，并测算了 GPU 租赁（ IaaS ）及模型 API 两类业务模式 —— 前者
可实现 60% ⾄ 70% 的边际 EBIT 利润率及 25% ⾄ 40% 的 ROIC ，后者增量 EBIT 利润率可超 70% ，
ROIC 最⾼可达 63% 。

### Page 3

AI 资本开⽀流向：谁在分⻝这块蛋糕
摩根⼠丹利通过⾃下⽽上的⽅式，将 1.58 万亿美元资本开⽀追踪⾄约 50 家公司的营收，覆盖科
技、通信服务、⼯业、能源及海外 ODM 等板块。按其测算，约 65% 的 AI 资本开⽀最终流⼊标普
500 成分股营收，其中英伟达占据流⼊规模的约三分之⼆。
在各受益板块中， AI 芯⽚、内存、⽹络及 ODM （原始设计制造商）获得最⼤份额，英伟达、三
星、 SK hynix 及美光合计拿⾛其中绝⼤部分。
芯⽚层⾯，摩根⼠丹利认为英伟达具备最佳⻛险回报⽐，理由是公司正处于新⼀代产品周期爬
坡早期， Fenyman 平台有望推动每 GW 算⼒对应营收超过 500 亿美元，且当前估值约 15 倍 FY28
EPS ，具备估值扩张空间。博通亦受益于定制硅业务扩张，预计 2028 年 XPU 市场规模将突破 1 万
亿美元。
内存层⾯，DRAM 已成为 AI 建设的关键瓶颈，但相关股票仅以约 3 倍 NTM 市盈率交易，隐含增⻓
溢价⼏乎为零。摩根⼠丹利判断内存周期将于 2026 年四季度进⼊后期，价格涨幅趋于温和，供

给增加将限制盈利惊喜空间。 SK hynix 与 Samsung 已通过⻓期协议（ LTA ）锁定⼤部分需求，

### Page 4

SK hynix 承诺回购并注销 400 亿韩元股份， Samsung 则设定 2026 年 900 亿⾄ 1100 亿韩元的股东
回报⽬标。
⽹络层⾯，每增加 1 美元超⼤规模云服务商资本开⽀，约对应 0.25 美元的⽹络营收，预计 2027 年
⽹络总营收约 3740 亿美元。在覆盖的主要公司中，英伟达（⽹络业务）、博通、 Innolight 及
Arista Networks 预计位居超⼤规模云相关⽹络营收前列。
设计软件层⾯，超⼤规模云服务商持续加⼤定制硅投⼊，为 Synopsys 和 Cadence 提供增量机
会，尤其是 Synopsys 新推出的应⽤优化 IP （ AOIP ）模式，通过⽣产版税将营收与芯⽚出货量更
直接挂钩。
数据中⼼建设引发的电⼒需求正成为⼯业及电⼒设备商的重要增⻓引擎。摩根⼠丹利指出， AI 资
本开⽀对⼯业板块的传导路径集中于电⼒供应与冷却设备。
电⼒设备⽅⾯，GE Vernova 燃⽓轮机已售罄⾄ 2029 年，售价从 2020 年代初的每千⽡ 1000 美元
涨⾄⽬前的每千⽡ 3000 美元，数据中⼼相关订单已占其管道订单的约三分之⼀。 INIO 与 BE 则分
别受益于⾼速往复式发动机及燃料电池需求。摩根⼠丹利认为，上调资本开⽀将进⼀步延伸电
⼒ OEM 的积压订单并提升利润率。
融资压⼒：⾼评级与低评级主体分化加剧
随着资本开⽀持续扩张，⾃由现⾦流缺⼝正在拉⼤。摩根⼠丹利估计，2027 年将是预测期内现
⾦流⾚字最⼤的⼀年。超⼤规模云服务商今年以来已发⾏约 2550 亿美元⽆担保债券，约占全年
预估资本开⽀的 30% ，但 10 年期债券全价收益率较 2025 年底上升约 100 ⾄ 180 个基点。
摩根⼠丹利将融资主体分为两个层次：⾼评级超⼤规模云服务商（亚⻢逊、 META 、⾕歌、微
软）⽬前利息覆盖倍数约 30.7 倍，即便 2027 年新增发债 4000 亿美元，覆盖倍数仍可维持在 11.9
倍，对融资成本基本不敏感；⽽低评级主体（甲⻣⽂、 SPCX ）的覆盖倍数仅约 4 倍，若同期新
增发债 1200 亿美元，覆盖倍数将降⾄约 1.8 倍，⾯临较⼤融资约束。
新兴云服务商（ neocloud ）主要依赖项⽬层⾯融资及可转债，是对融资成本最敏感的群体，通
常需要借助⾼评级交易对⼿的信⽤⽀持来获取融资。
从融资结构演变看，摩根⼠丹利预计 2027 年发债期限将向短端倾斜，芯⽚和服务器因现⾦流产
⽣速度较快，更适合以资产⽀持融资及私募形式匹配，具备与合同需求挂钩的灵活性。

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

4 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Exhibit 2: Charting the “Yentervention” and U.S. Treasury Yields Surge Across the Curve.
A) The Yen Regains Some Strength. B) 5-Year Forward Yields Hold Above 5%.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.
Investment Implications
While some key variables are
complicating the macro backdrop
(i.e., higher-for-longer yields/oil)
the combination of strong earnings
growth, robust consumer demand
(real final sales to private domestic
purchasers grew at 4.6% for Q2) and
ongoing capital investment suggests
that the fundamental backdrop for
U.S. Equities remains constructive.

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

(PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for the Euro Area)

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

### Page 6

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG

In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts
0
1

3
4

1970 1980 1990 2000 2010 2020 2030 2040 2050
Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

Figure 7: Gold Prices (US Dollars per ounce, log scale)

### Page 8

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)
-2
0
2
4

16
1955 1960 1965 1970 1975 1980 1985
First Oil Shock
Second Oil Shock
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Source : NBER, Deutsche Bank
Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

Core weakness outweighs progress in AI optics
CPO progress is more tangible but substantial success is already priced in
We maintain Sell and our NT$5,000 target price as erosion in the core smartphone
business outweighs CPO progress. The Q326 call confirmed that Largan's fiber array has
passed customer qualification although its customer qualified another vendor's prism
micro-lens assembly (PMLA) that Largan would now buy in. If Largan qualifies the full

FAU plus carrier assembly for 2H27, content rises but with more pass-through cost and
less margin upside. We roll our valuation to 2028E, the first full year of CPO production,
though 25% below the NT$6,660 close on October 8.
Full FAU assembly would lift content, but with more pass-through
We believe Largan's FA customer is one of two  module suppliers on NVIDIA's 2H27
program, which we estimate could reach 40% share. The program is still subject to

successful yield ramp and could move to metalens in 2028, which would reopen supplier
qualification. Assembling the full FAU could lift Largan's ASP to US$125-150 leveraging
its precision alignment and automation but the PMLA, V-groove and carrier parts would
be pass through that cuts into margin leverage. We expand our FAU TAM analysis
(Figure 9S u m m a r y  o f  F A U  d r i v e r s :  1 2 . 3 m n / 3 5 . 6 m n  b a s e  c a s e  i n  2 0 2 8 - 2 0 3 0 ) with FAU market in 2028/30 expanding to 12.3mn/35.6mn units, with Largan

base case set at 25% of the TAM.
Core smartphone business stays soft into 2027
Q326 sales of NT$15.7bn, +15% QoQ/-11% YoY missedUBSe/Street +36%/+33% on

after a pricier iPhone 18 launch and possible lag to Genius which is picking up periscope
share and outgrew in Q3 (+1% YoY vs. -11% YoY). We cut 2027 from NT$77bn to NT

Maintain Sell and our NT$5,000 target
We cut 2026E/27E EPS by 14%/16% to NT$160/NT$180 and introduce 2028E EPS of

NT$220 for the core smartphone business. We value the lens business at NT$3,300 (15x
2028E ex-CPO EPS of NT$220, the middle of Largan's 13–18x historical range) and CPO
option at NT$1,700 or US$7bn (49x our base-case 2028E CPO EPS of NT$35, near the
top of optical peers' 14–54x). Our base case gives Largan 25% of a 12.3mn-unit, US
$1.85bn FAU market at US$150 per unit and a 50% gross margin. That credits it with
yields and margins it still needs to prove in a quarter when yields and energy costs cut

7.2ppts from its lens margin. At ~NT$6,660, the shares imply ~NT$69 of 2028E CPO
EPS, about twice our base case.

CPO fiber array qualified, but rising pass-through as it upgrades to FAU
production
Largan's fiber array (FA) has passed qualification with a volume customer, and
management expects mass-production capacity by mid-2027 in a newly completed fab
dedicated to FA/FAU, using in-house equipment. The company's next step is to produce
the full detachable FAU mounted on a carrier, a board with three alignment beams that
sits on the optical engine's receptor. Largan can build FAUs now and says the customer

### Page 3

Largan Precision 12 October 2026 ab 3
would prefer it to supply the full assembly, but qualification is pending with the
customer preferring FAU and carrier sampled separately.
Even as its potential to supply the full FAU improve, Largan will buy in most of the
content. The customer has qualified another PMLA vendor and prefers semiconductor-
made optics; V-grooves and carrier beams and boards are outsourced and the customer
does not want Largan to make MT ferrules. Management described its role as closer to a

contractor and said margins hinge on yields, as the FA carries a high ASP but also a large
bill of materials.  Supplying the complete FAU could raise billed content, but a larger
purchased bill of materials also dilutes the relationship between revenue and value
added. Our full-FAU ASP assumption requires Largan to capture that invoice scope.
Next year's product is grating-coupled and works for both CPO and NPO while edge-
coupled designs are being explored with the customer. The current product does not go

directly to end customers, but management expects to work directly with end customers
on next-generation designs (likely 2028) as it progresses toward multi-row FAs.
To ramp production, Largan has bought several land parcels and will build new fabs from
scratch, with construction finishing around 2030. It may outsource some capacity in the
interim and said it cannot yet be sure capacity will meet demand.
Annual FAU TAM reaches US$1.85bn in 2028 and US$4.5bn in 2030 in

our base case
We update our FAU (fiber array unit) TAM factoring CPO/NPO penetration into
AI networking switches, NVIDIA multi-rack systems, scale-up on compute and switch
boards, and non-NVIDIA accelerators.  We assume one passive FAU assembly connecting
a fiber array to a photonic engine with one unit per optical engine.
We assume one FAU per optical engine and 32 per switch ASIC in our base case,
matching NVIDIA's 32x3.2T engines on its 102.4T Spectrum-X package. Broadcom's

102.4Tb/s Davisson uses 16 engines at 6.4Tb/s, so our bear case assumes and even mix
of these two designs (24 per ASIC). We note that higher switch bandwidth does not
automatically increase FAU count if engine bandwidth rises at the same rate. Our 2030
assumption maintains the 32 FAUs per switch based on bandwidth rising to 204.8Tb/s
ASICs using 6.4Tb/s optical engines.

Our base case for the FAU market sums up 4 opportunities for CPO/NPO attach:
1. Scale-out and scale-across switches
We estimate annual switch ASIC shipments x CPO + NPO penetration x optical engines
per ASIC x FAUs per engine. In 2028, 25% CPO/NPO penetration or 1.0m 102.4T switch
ASICs at 32 FAUs each produces 8.0m FAUs. In 2030, 55% of 1.1mn 204.8T switch
ASICs at 32 FAUs drives a market size of 19.4mn FAUs.

2. NVIDIA's multi-rack connections for Rubin Ultra and Feynman
NVIDIA at its 2026 GTC San Jose proposed a multi-rack option to network together 8
Oberon NVL72 racks for the Rubin Ultra generation and 8 Kyber NVL144 racks.

Feedback at the time showed  possible transceiver or NPO connections for Rubin Ultra
and CPO connections for Feynman.  Our 2028 base case uses 110,000 racks, 15% multi-
rack adoption and 75% CPO/NPO penetration, giving approximately 12,400 connected
racks.
For 2030, our rack count falls to 35,000 racks as we assume Kyber would have 4x the
number of GPUs, equivalent to 140k Oberon equivalents. We assume the 35,000 racks
at 55% multi-rack adoption x 80% CPO/NPO attach gives 15,400 connected racks. We

assume about 216 and 576 inter-rack FAUs per rack, respectively, driving a market
opportunity of 2.7mn FAUs in 2028 and 8.9mn in 2030.

We sum four pools: scale-out/scale-across switches, NVIDIA multi-rack pods, CPO native

Scenario analysis on Largan's CPO opportunity
Our base case gives Largan the benefit of the doubt for successfully ramping up a full
FAU production and capturing 25% of the overall FAU TAM, yielding NT$35 of 2028E
EPS, about 16% of our 2028E lens EPS. A 25% share means 3.1mn FAUs in its first full
year of production: about 29% of NVIDIA-linked units and roughly three-quarters of the

allocation our checks suggest its module partner holds. We book these at the full US
$150 FAU price and 50% gross margin, allowing for an early-supply premium even
though the optics, V-grooves and carrier parts are bought in. That gives 2028E CPO sales
of US$462mn, rising to US$1.1bn and NT$84 of EPS in 2030 on a larger market.
Our bear case (15% share, 45% GM) gives NT$5 of CPO EPS in 2028E and NT$14 in
2030E; our bull case (35% share, 55% GM) gives NT$103 and NT$267. Each 5ppt of

share moves 2028E CPO EPS by ~NT$6.9 (~NT$340/share at 49x), and each 5ppt of
gross margin by ~NT$4.6 (~NT$225/share).  If gross margin were 35% rather than 50%,
holding operating expenses at 12% of sales, EPS would fall to approximately NT$21. A
next-generation shift in lens or coupling design could also change content and
alignment requirements including a potential metalens design which could invite in
different suppliers.
Our 2030 base case implies ~8.9mn Largan FAUs a year (21mn in the upside case),

though with risks around competition in a crowded field, yields and technology. A shift
to semiconductor-made optics or metalens would loosen alignment tolerances and
erode the precision premium behind Largan's pitch. The upside lies in direct work with
cloud providers on multi-row FAs from 2028, where Largan's multi-layer alignment is
most differentiated.

Valuation and investment implications
We roll our valuation forward to 2028E and value Largan on a sum of the parts. We

value the lens business at NT$3,300 (15x 2028E EPS, the middle of Largan's 13–18x
historical range) and CPO at NT$1,700 (49x our base-case 2028E CPO EPS of NT$35,
near the top of optical peers' 14–54x). Our base case gives Largan 25% of a 12.3mn-
unit, US$1.85bn FAU market at US$150 per unit and a 50% gross margin. The 49x
multiple also assigns a substantial value to a business whose complete assembly still
requires qualification and whose margins still need to demonstrate can reach similar

levels to its mature smartphone business. At ~NT$6,660 recent share price level, the
shares imply ~NT$69 of 2028E CPO EPS, about twice our base case.
Upside risks include faster full-FAU qualification, firm volume commitments from
additional customers, stronger lens demand and better yields. Near-term catalysts are
the customer's capacity forecast and its visit to the new fab in the coming month. A
large forecast or faster FAU plus carrier qualification would be upside risks to our sell.

Forecast returns

Market return assumption 6.3%
Forecast excess return -30.0%
Company Description
Founded in 1987, Largan Precision (Largan) is one of the world's leading manufacturers of
lenses used in cameras and IT products. It specialises in plastic/hybrid lenses and, to a lesser
extent, glass lenses and modules. In 2022, 20MP+ camera lenses were 10-20% of Largan's

shipment mix, 10MP lenses were 50-60%, 8MP lenses were 0-10% and other lenses were
30-40%.
Valuation Method and Risk Statement
Our price target is based on SOTP valuation on the CPO opportunity and traditional lens
business.
We think Largan's downside risks include: 1) ongoing pricing pressure from smartphone OEM
clients and increasingly intense competition among lens suppliers; and 2) a slower-than-
expected smartphone demand recovery in China, and 3) CPO commercial success below the

market's elevated expectations.
We believe the key upside risk is a faster-than-expected CPO qualification process at Largan.
In addition, if the company demonstrates exceptionally efficient and highly accurate
automation capabilities, it could support greater market share gains within the industry than
we currently anticipate.

near term, usually in response to an event that may affect the investment case or valuation. Equity Price Targets have an investment
horizon of 12 months.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read

the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any

issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is
not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment
to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and,

if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured
investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ
from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation

before investing in any securities.

Document 322: United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026

world
A SHOCK-RESISTANT EXPANSION
US growth remains resilient despite successive shocks. According to our forecasts, it is expected to reach 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027. Its main driver is still investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has remained entirely unaffected by the
energy crisis. The labour market has seen a marked improvement in job creation and a fall in the unemployment rate. Core

inflation remains stubbornly high and above the Federal Reserve (Fed)’s target. Against this backdrop, the Fed has begun a
hiking cycle in September, which, according to our scenario, would bring the Fed Funds target to 4.50% (upper limit) by Q1
2027. At the same time, long-term rates are under upward pressure.
1  This includes: energy and communications; data centres (facilities); IT equipment and peripherals; communication equipment (equipment); software; and research and develop-

ment (intellectual-property products).
THE ENERGY SHOCK IS NOT HOLDING BACK GROWTH
Driven by AI-related investment, US growth is holding up despite ri -
sing energy inflation. We forecast GDP growth at 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027 on an annual average basis. The AI-related investment
cycle1 (+14.8% y/y in H1 2026) is expected to continue, accompanied by
potential productivity gains. However, other private-investment seg -
ments (including residential property) remain sluggish. In addition, the

rising cost of capital could accentuate its ‘K-shaped’ profile. Household
consumption is expected, on the whole, to benefit from the rebound in
job creation and to remain above 2% y/y through to the end of 2027.
In H1 2026, it held up against resurging inflation due to an average
year-on-year increase of 11.5% in tax refunds and a savings rate stan-
ding at its lowest level since 2022 (2.8% in Q2).
RECORD CAPITAL-RAISING DRIVEN BY AI
This year, AI-related investments are driving massive capital-raising

by companies through loans, bond issues or share placements. This
trend is set to continue given the seemingly colossal scale of the fun -
ding requirements, even as risk premia in the bond market begin to
widen. According to their statements, the five tech giants could invest
nearly USD 900 bn this year and over USD 1,000 bn next year—more
than double the figure for 2025. Spurred on by regulatory easing, the
major US banks have announced that they will mobilise significant re-

sources to support projects considered strategic for the United States
(AI, defence, energy and infrastructure). While funding for the AI boom
appears unaffected by rising long-term interest rates, these latter are
likely to continue to weigh on household demand for mortgages. Since
the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 30-year mortgage rates
have risen by 78 bp (to 6.76% at the start of September). Conversely,
consumer lending remained buoyant in H1 2026 and is supporting

household spending.
TWO-SPEED GROWTH IN INDUSTRY
Industrial production rebounded slightly in 2025 (+1.1%), driven by AI
and aerospace. We forecast an acceleration to +1.5% in 2026 and +2%
in 2027. Investment in AI is bolstering the electronics sector, while the
(civil and military) aerospace sector is rebounding as order backlogs
are cleared and defence requirements rise. Conversely, sectors ex -
posed to rising tariffs (in particular, the steel, aluminium and auto -

motive sectors) are still on the back burner, contrary to the objective
of these measures. This positive momentum in industrial production,
while concentrated, is expected to continue, bolstered by the recove -
ry in business sentiment. The ISM Manufacturing Index has been in
expansionary territory since early 2026, despite ongoing pressures on
prices and supply chains.
